#2110. A failed spell at Wolves almost saw him walk away from football ...now Danny Armstrong is idolised in Romania as he attempts to lead Dinamo Bucharest back to former glories

Published: 13:42 EST, 9 November 2026 | Updated: 13:52 EST, 9 November 2026

The daughter says: ‘Da’. Darby Armstrong, all of two years old, is not calling out for her father, Danny.

‘She is saying “Yes” in Romanian. She can count to ten in Romanian,’ says her proud father. She may have to learn to count higher, and quickly, to chart her father’s figures in the Superliga with Dinamo Bucharest.

Armstrong sits in an upmarket restaurant in the beautiful suburbs of Bucharest with a park stretching towards a lake. He sips ginger tea with honey and engages with waiters, diners and children who approach him for a chat and a photograph.

‘Darby is a bit bemused by it,’ he says. ‘We were in the mall there and a guy wanted a pic. I could see her looking up to see what all the fuss was about.’

Armstrong could be forgiven for being ever so slightly bemused himself. At 29, he has taken a few dunts and not just from enthusiastic full-backs. Success has hardly been overnight.

‘I nearly gave up once,’ he says quietly. ‘Another time I thought it was all over and I was looking at one-to-one coaching or being a personal trainer.’

Danny Armstrong is hailed after his match-winning heroics in a crucial win over UTA Arad

He survived, though. He is a hero at Dinamo. There is a clamour on matchday when he comes out of the tunnel. The ultras, high behind a goal, chant his name with a fervour that suddenly breaks out in red flares and manic jumping.

It is a long way from Ayr. Armstrong knows he did not walk it alone. His saviours include John McGlynn, who brought him to Raith Rovers, George Cairns, who convinced him to stay at Hamilton, and Scott Sellars, the youth coach who advised him to leave Wolverhampton Wanderers for first team football.

He also took inspiration from many team-mates, including Kieran Tierney who was once his room-mate in a Scotland under-age team,

‘I was meant to be rooming with another left-back but he pulled out and this young player from Celtic came in,’ recalls Armstrong. ‘He wasn’t even in the squad initially and now he is Scotland’s best left-back alongside Robbo.

‘I used to play against KT so many times and I have great respect for him, playing so many games for Celtic, getting that move to Arsenal and coming back to Scotland to win trophies. The setbacks he has had… he’s an inspiration.

‘He has resilience. He has motivation. He took the half opportunity that was offered to him and ran with it.’

Armstrong has done the same in Bucharest. He has been bolstered by his past. ‘It builds you,’ he says of his experience. ‘There have been tough spells but you can look back and make sense of them and see how they have helped you. Football takes a lot of work.’

Armstrong has thrived in Romania since making the leftfield switch to Dinamo Bucharest

It takes a lot of belief too. That can be a capricious quality, seemingly dependent on the winds of fortune.

Armstrong can point to two hat-tricks that gave him faith in a time of need. ‘I scored a hat-trick for Hamilton in a youth game at Auchenhowie against Rangers,’ he says. ‘That came after I had been convinced to stick at it.’

He also scored three last season in the derby against Steaua. ‘That was crazy,’ he says. ‘I don’t think I have realised even yet how big it was. People tell me it was the greatest day in their lives. We hadn’t beaten them in five or six years. We were also in a bad spot with poor form. And then I score three. It gives me goosebumps even now.’

He adds: ‘There is a real football culture here. It means so much to people and I love that. It actually wasn’t too hard to come here because I had looked at it a year earlier but stayed on at Kilmarnock. When Dinamo came in and showed me videos and talked about the history, I was on board.’

There was one decisive factor. ‘I knew it was time to try to make a name for myself somewhere else. I wanted to test myself, keep myself open to other opportunities, try to get to the highest level I can get.’

He is professionally coy about the prospect of international football, having again missed out on Sebastien Pocognoli’s latest Scotland squad. He maintains the line of concentrating on his club form, having a dream to play for his country, and hoping to be noticed.

Armstrong was a big hit at Kilmarnock and has long been mooted for a Scotland call-up

His performances in Romania, though, have been so outstanding that there may be the odd glance from the Pocognoli camp at just what is happening at Dinamo. They may have to be quick as Armstrong qualifies for Ireland too.

He plays in a wide right role, with the freedom to cut inside and become a playmaker. His manager, Nuno Campos, has faith in him. He is the latest in a line of characters who have led him from doubt to conviction.

There is no hesitation. ‘It was McGlynn who saved me,’ says Armstrong. But there were previous tribulations that required the intervention of football folk and Armstrong is immensely grateful.

‘I had been going up to Hamilton from Ayr every night as a kid, playing at the weekend, and by the time I was about 15 I think I was drained. I felt I was missing out on other things in life. It was tough on my parents, too, who did everything for me. The family always sacrificed themselves for me.’

His parents told George Cairns, Hamilton coach, that their son did not want to continue. The reply was instant: ‘Get him up here and I will have a chat with him.’

The crisis passed after this talk and that hat-trick against Rangers. But further tribulations were to come.

Armstrong's relentless drive and enthusiasm have made him a standout player in Romania

Armstrong headed to Wolverhampton Wanderers, spurning an offer by Rangers under Mark Warburton and Davie Weir. ‘That was a mistake,’ he says. He was never near first team football at Wolves though the club wanted to retain him. After a chat with Scott Sellars, he took the chance to leave and start again.

‘That was very, very difficult,’ he says. ‘I did not play from May to November. I was sitting by the phone, urging it to ring with an offer. I had thoughts about packing it all in and going into coaching or being a personal trainer.’

Then an unlikely Superman came to the rescue in the shape of McGlynn, then Raith manager. ‘That changed everything, everything,’ says Armstrong.

A bounce game was organised to assess the player but Armstrong admits the team’s performance was awful. ‘I was very much part of that,’ he says.

He did much better in another friendly but budget considerations meant McGlynn could offer only a nine-game contract. ‘It was a couple of hundred quid a week but it meant everything to me.’ Five goals in nine games led to an extended contract.

‘He is a genius,’ says Armstrong. ‘He is so big on detail, detail, detail. He is a fantastic football coach. It’s an obsession for him.

‘I have never met a man like him. He saved my football career and made me believe I could make it.’

John McGlynn helped save Armstrong's career by taking him under his wing at Raith Rovers

After a spell at Ross County, Armstrong flourished under Derek McInnes at Kilmarnock and the move to Romania offered further scope for growth.

‘It was a bit difficult at first settling in but now my partner, Lauren, and Darby love it. We have made friends,’ he says.

At this a couple of waiters approach the table with grins and cheery banter. Armstrong responds with a warm handshake and that half-embrace that signals another acquiescence to yet another picture.

‘The big difference this season is the coach,’ says Armstrong. ‘He has come in and completely changed the environment and the atmosphere. He has brought fun back and he makes you want to come into training.’

Dinamo are now sitting joint-top of the league. ‘The first goal has to be European football, getting this club back to where it belongs,’ he says.

Armstrong has his sights set on an historic Romanian title and is not ruling out a longer stay

The Dinamo fans, who have not witnessed a league title in 20 years, sense Superliga glory. ‘We are a good side and we play nice stuff,’ says Armstrong. ‘But let’s take it game by game.’

He has the same laid-back attitude to his contract. ‘It expires at the end of the season and there is an option there but we leave all that to after Christmas.’

He is happy in Bucharest but knows the fate of a footballer is never predictable. ‘It is a wonderful place. The fans are great and the manager has faith in me.’

He points out that Campos wants him to be a ‘monster’. This is an instruction to be powerful and innovative in the final third.

The next night Armstrong swivels just outside the box and hits a wonderful shot to open the scoring against UTA Arad.

He then sprints towards the stand where Lauren and Darby are celebrating. He has just been a monster. But he is still a dad, in whatever language.

#2108. Careless Liverpool concede same goal twice in second-half capitulation against Leeds

Leeds United came from 2-0 down at half-time to beat Liverpool 4-2 at Soldier Field in Chicago as Andoni Iraola’s side were undone by sloppy defending in the second period.

Liverpool’s dominant first-half display was underlined by goals from Luke Chambers and Florian Wirtz. However, Daniel Farke’s men replied with four strikes in 25 second-half minutes through Sean Longstaff, Brenden Aaronson and two alarmingly similar goals from Dominic Calvert-Lewin.

🎯 "CALVERT-LEWIN ARRIVES!" pic.twitter.com/CaHOeXQn7E

🤩 "Calvert-Lewin makes it FOUR!" pic.twitter.com/mEEi882OWi

Liverpool centre-back Chambers, 22, who spent last season on loan at Charlton Athletic, opened the scoring on seven minutes, pouncing on a Dominik Szoboszlai corner which had been flicked on at the near post by Milos Kerkez.

Jeremie Frimpong, serviced by a wonderfully flighted cross-field ball by Trey Nyoni, then set up Wirtz to make it 2-0 five minutes before the break, but on the hour Leeds began their recovery when Aaronson scored following a mistake by Kerkez – who gave up possession to Ethan Ampadu in his own half.

Ampadu’s long throw was flicked on to Calvert-Lewin to level the score, then minutes later Curtis Jones’s carelessness allowed Longstaff in to put Leeds in front.

🔥 "LONGSTAFF! WHAT A TURNAROUND!" pic.twitter.com/G9LvLIk0Mi

And it was a familiar story five minutes from time when Ampadu’s throw again prompted chaos in Liverpool’s back line, allowing Calvert-Lewin to steal in front of Jones and seal victory.

Liverpool return to Anfield for friendlies against Monaco and Como before they begin their Premier League season away at Newcastle United later this month.

Leeds have matches against RB Leipzig, Manchester United and Augsburg before their league campaign gets under way at Nottingham Forest.

Aaronson is booked for a cynical pull on Ramsay.

Koumas should score for Liverpool. He is played in behind and for some strange reason Cairns comes a long way out of his goal. Koumas should lift it over Cairns and into the empty net but gets his chip wrong and the shot goes off target.

Leeds’ second half gets even better and Liverpool’s gets even worse. It is a very similar goal to Calvert-Lewin’s first; Ampadu’s long throw is flicked on and Calvert-Lewin meets it, heading past Mamardashvili. This is very much your game of two halves.

Daniel Farke is making a double change as Gnonto and Lienou come on.

Frimpong kicks the ball out of play and he may have a problem. Is it just cramp or more serious than that? He is going to come off and be replaced by Scanlon.

Leeds have come from 2-0 down to lead 3-2 in Chicago. Aaronson’s cross from the left side of the box should be cleared by Jones but he does not put his foot through it. Instead, that allows Longstaff to slot home past Mamardashvili. Leeds have scored three goals in a quarter of an hour.

Leeds are on level terms. A long throw from Ampadu out on the left is flicked on at the near post and Calvert-Lewin has the easiest of tap-ins at the back post. Liverpool were in cruise control at 2-0 up at the break but not have not been at it since half-time.

Justin is played in behind down the left channel and tries to pick out Calvert-Lewin in the centre of the box but Ndiaye steps in with a crucial interception.

Andoni Iraola is going to make a quadruple change. Elliott, Ramsay, Ndiaye and Tsimikas all come on. Szoboszlai is one of those to head off and hands Jones the armband.

Liverpool were much the better side in the first half but have not really been at the races since the break.

Leeds punish Kerkez’s error and get a goal back. Kerkez gifts the ball away inside his own half and Ampadu picks up not far outside the Liverpool penalty area. He spots the run of Aaronson to his left and slides it into the American, who takes one touch before sliding it underneath Mamardashvili and into the Liverpool net.

Wilson is fouled down Leeds’ right and he will take the free-kick. The Welshman clips it in and picks out Calvert-Lewin, whose header goes well wide of the target.

Calvert-Lewin gets a touch past Ndukwe just inside the Liverpool half and drives towards the Liverpool penalty area. It is difficult to know whether it is a shot or a cross from Calvert-Lewin and the ball goes wide of the far post. Bogle did make a run towards the back post and was hoping Calvert-Lewin would pick him out.

Liverpool negotiate a couple of long throws and a corner from Leeds in quick succession.

After Ndukwe’s pass inside his own half is intercepted, Justin decides to shoot from range but Mamardashvili easily saves.

Liverpool have made a number of changes at the break, with Chiesa, Jones, Gravenberch and Koumas all coming on. It takes an absolute age for Leeds to re-emerge, with Liverpool coming back out from the tunnel nearly ten minutes before Leeds. What on earth is going on in the Leeds dressing room at the break? This is ridiculous from Leeds.

The second half begins more than 20 minutes after the first ended. To be exact, 23 minutes is the gap. Calvert-Lewin is one of a few changes at half-time for Leeds, as is former Liverpool man Wilson. We are back under way in Chicago.

Leeds have been forced into one change in this half and they might be forced into another here. As Wirtz scored, two Leeds players collided, which has resulted in Ampadu staying down and receiving treatment. The Leeds captain though does seem to be ok after receiving some treatment.

It is a lovely team goal from Liverpool to take them 2-0 up around five minutes before half-time. Nyoni’s flat ball out to the right side of the box is volleyed first time back across the box by Frimpong. Wirtz runs onto the ball and, from the penalty spot, slots the ball home past Cairns.

Ngumoha’s step-over takes him past Bogle inside the Leeds penalty area and he drives the ball across the six-yard box but no-one in a Liverpool shirt is on hand to tap it in. Szoboszlai, who loves scoring free-kick stunners, blasts the free-kick into the wall.

Before the free-kick will be taken, Leeds look like they will be forced into a change as Gudmundsson has gone down clutching at his right hamstring.

Liverpool counter from their own box after defending a long throw through Isak and Frimpong, whose cross from the right is deflected behind for the first corner of the game...

Both sides emerge from the tunnel and we are just moments away from kick-off at Soldier Field in Chicago. You’ll Never Walk Alone rings around the stadium as the teams emerge.

There was a lot to take in, during those madcap 42 seconds, but it was a scene that showed the full extent of the issues Andoni Iraola must address.

First came a handshake with Curtis Jones that the midfielder, lost in red mist, barely acknowledged. It was not impressive, shrugging off a new manager like that, but as Iraola strode across the field at Yankee Stadium, after Liverpool’s 1-0 win over Wrexham on Wednesday, the action really began.

Jones squawked and jabbed at his arm. Dominik Szoboszlai bellowed back, arms stretched out so wide you could not help but notice. Next came Kostas Tsimikas throwing the captain’s armband on the floor, landing at Jones’s feet, as Giorgi Mamardashvili and Jeremie Frimpong looked on awkwardly.

Soldier Field in Chicago hosts an all-Premier League tie encounter as Liverpool take on Leeds as both sides bring their US tours to a close. Both Premier League encounters between these sides last season ended in draws; a 3-3 draw at Elland Road was followed up by a 0-0 stalemate at Anfield. The draw at Elland Road was immediately followed by Mohamed Salah speaking to the media, claiming he had been thrown under the bus.

It is a new era at Liverpool as former Bournemouth boss Andoni Iraola has replaced Arne Slot at Anfield. They kicked off the Iraola era in Nashville, Tennessee, with a 4-2 win over Sunderland, following that up with another victory in New York on Wednesday night, beating Wrexham 1-0, and will be aiming to make it three wins from three on their US tour.

“I think it [the US tour] has been really, really useful,” Iraola said in an interview on the official Liverpool website. Obviously, you spend a lot of time with the players, with the staff, you get all the information you can. And I think there is also an evolution, it has to be. We start to understand each other better, what we need to do. Every game, I hope we can get closer and closer and put ourselves in a position where the few players that still are not here start to train also, they are helped by the others.”

Virgil van Dijk and Cody Gakpo resumed training back on Merseyside whilst Alexis Mac Allister, Alisson and Victor Munoz are yet to return to training following their World Cup exploits. The trio of Florian Wirtz, Alexander Isak and Ryan Gravenberch are all set to feature for the first time this pre-season.

Leeds’ pre-season campaign began with a 3-2 loss to Championship Wrexham in Tampa, Florida before beating Sunderland 1-0 in New Jersey courtesy of a long-range strike from Lukas Nmecha. Former Liverpool man Harry Wilson joined Leeds on a free from Fulham this summer.

“Really tough but that is expected,” Wilson said. That is what pre-season is for, building up a base for when the season starts, making sure you are in the best physical position you can be to attack the season. It has been good. I played against them [Leeds] twice last year when I was at Fulham. Both games were really, really tough games.

“I think when the fans have got a good team to support, I think that [the atmosphere at Elland Road] goes up a notch. I think the size of the club was a massive part of me coming. Then speaking to the guys, the chairman, the sporting director, and seeing kind of the plans and the aims for the club, it is something I wanted to be a part of.”

#2107. Domani il Milan torna ad allenarsi, si rivede Pavlovic. Il punto su Tomori e Loftus-Cheek

Dopo il derby, Stra­hi­nja Pavlo­vic si è arreso ai pro­blemi fisici, ha sal­tato l'ultima tra­sferta di Genova e la pre­ce­dente gara casa­linga con­tro il Feren­c­va­ros in Europa Lea­gue. Ma in que­sti giorni il serbo (che ha sal­tato anche la con­vo­ca­zione in nazio­nale) ha lavo­rato per recu­pe­rare e pre­sen­tarsi in gruppo domani alla ripresa della pre­pa­ra­zione del Milan.

#2106. Belgio, van Bommel in conferenza stamp: “Italia? Non mi ha sorpreso, magari la Francia l’ha sottovalutata”

Marc van Bommel, commissario tecnico del Belgio, ha rilasciato alcune dichiarazioni in conferenza stampa, alla vigilia del match contro l’Italia: “Domani conta solo la vittoria, ma non è una finale che ti consente di vincere una coppa. E’ una partita importante che ci consentirebbe di continuare o no. È un test comparabile ad altre coppe, una partita interessante da giocare sotto pressione. Noi ci prepariamo sempre nello stesso modo, che sia una finale o no. contro la Francia. L’Italia è un’ottima squadra, magari la Francia l’ha presa sotto gamba, ma non mi stupisce? Lukaku è sempre importante, dall’inizio o da riserva È un giocatore ci grande esperienza ed è una buona cosa avere in uno stadio pieno”.

#2105. Liam Scales has no doubt 'amazing' Kasper Hogh will soon bring an end to his worrying Celtic goal drought

By JOHN MCGARRY, SCOTTISH CHIEF SPORTS FEATURE WRITER

Published: 14:09 EST, 9 November 2026 | Updated: 14:54 EST, 9 November 2026

Liam Scales claims he’s got no fears about Kasper Hogh’s luck turning after the Dane’s goalless run at Celtic extended to 16 matches.

Without a goal to his name since hitting a hat-trick at Kilmarnock on August 9, the forward thought his drought was over when he found the net against the same opponent on Sunday.

Denied his long-awaited moment of glory when VAR spotted an earlier infringement by Mika Baur, Hogh watched from the bench as Martin O’Neill’s side ground out a one-goal win which took them four points clear at the top of the table.

But while the Dane will now have to wait until the home match with St Mirren following the international break for his next opportunity, Scales – the scorer on the day – feels his team-mate has nothing to worry about.

‘He’s been brilliant,’ said the Irishman. ‘He keeps doing the right things and eventually it will fall for him.

‘He was so unlucky with his goal being ruled out. And he told me he was right behind me with the goal I scored. If I’d known that I might have left it!

Liam Scales was Celtic's weekend goal hero but he is tipping Kasper Hogh to come good

‘It will fall for him. He’s a goalscorer and a good player. His all-round game was brilliant, he held the ball up really well.

‘He’s just been unlucky. But his attitude has been brilliant. If he keeps doing the right things it will work out for him.

‘He has an amazing attitude and that’s why I really believe he will be successful.’

With Benjamin Nygren missing a second-half penalty, O’Neill was grateful for the improvised finish of Scales earlier in the game.

‘It took an age to come down,’ Scales recalled of his third of the season. ‘I wasn’t sure if it was going to land on the roof of the net.

‘I was just trying to guide it on target and luckily enough it went in. It was nice to score and hopefully I can get a few more between now and the end of the season.’

The victory over Killie ensured Celtic bounced back from their loss at Falkirk and a draw with Dundee United and built on their dramatic midweek win over Besiktas in the Europa League.

Hogh thought he had ended his scoring drought at Rugby Park but his effort was ruled out

‘I do think confidence is a massive part of our group and it was probably hit a little bit. The win against a top side like Besiktas has boosted us a little bit. There’s kind of a better mood around and we just want to keep adding good results.’

Scales is optimistic that the better options that are now at the manager’s disposal can help the side hit the kind of form which has eluded them this season.

#2102. Falkirk boss John McGlynn deserves big move, says Craig Levein, as Aberdeen sack Stephen Robinson

By JOHN MCGARRY, SCOTTISH CHIEF SPORTS FEATURE WRITER

Published: 17:30 EST, 9 November 2026 | Updated: 17:30 EST, 9 November 2026

Craig Levein believes John McGlynn ‘ticks every box’ to manage a bigger club than Falkirk — as Aberdeen begin the search for Stephen Robinson’s successor.

The Northern Irishman was sacked last night after eight months in charge, with the Dons’ 4-0 home loss to Hibs on Saturday proving to be the last straw.

With the Pittodrie side now sitting second bottom of the Premiership, chairman Dave Cormack, sporting director Lutz Pfannenstiel and newly-appointed chief executive Adrian Bevington will soon begin the process of trying to identify the man they believe can take the team forward.

And Levein, a former Hearts colleague of McGlynn’s, believes the veteran’s record of taking the Bairns from League One to third place in the top flight should speak for itself.

'John has passed every test that’s been put in front of him and I don’t know what he needs to do,’ said Levein.

‘Falkirk supporters, of course, won’t like me saying that, but I’ve worked with the guy. He’s got integrity, knowledge, he delves into the minutiae when he’s doing his team talks and makes sure everybody knows what’s expected.

Falkirk boss John McGlynn has guided them from League One to third in the Premiership

Aberdeen boss Stephen Robinson was sacked after eight months in charge at Pittodrie

‘I’m biased, I like the guy, but I think he’s done a wonderful job. He’s done all the work that he needs to do in my eyes to get an opportunity.’

McGlynn worked at Hearts as a coach under Jim Jefferies and went on to manage the Gorgie club twice — once as an interim.

Prior to taking charge of Falkirk in 2022, he had two spells at Raith Rovers, a stint at Livingston and a scouting role at Celtic under Brendan Rodgers.

Despite completely turning the Bairns’ fortunes around, Levein believes that there remains a strange reluctance in some quarters to recognise the 64-year-old as one of the shrewdest managers in the country.

‘I don’t know what it is, looking at what he’s done, his whole body of work,’ shrugged Levein.

‘When I went to Hearts at the tail end of my career, I was helping John do some training with the youngsters. Even then, you could tell immediately that he knows what he’s talking about.

‘He spent a fair bit of time with Brendan at Celtic. After that, I think he adopted Brendan’s style of football with some changes and altered two or three bits.

‘Essentially, he went to Celtic, watched and learned everything on top of the knowledge he already had. His team at Raith started to play in the same way as Celtic did. Then he’s continued that when he’s gone to Falkirk.

‘I was promoting him for the Scotland job when it came around. If you look at his record, it’s pretty impressive.

‘I just think he is undervalued, maybe not by the supporters that have had him as their manager, but certainly in a wider sense in Scotland. He has done a brilliant job so far.

‘I’ll come back to the fact I’m biased. I like the guy. I like what he does at football clubs. If I’m a chairman, all day long I’d be looking for somebody like him.

‘He’s meticulous in his work and everything’s done properly. He stays within the bandwidth of emotions.

‘He doesn’t get overly emotional during matches and he stays in his area, which I think is good for him and good for his players.

‘You don’t often see him throwing stuff about in the dugout or anything like that. He’s a top manager who has improved enormously in the last five or six years.’

Craig Levein believes McGlynn has done more than enough to merit a move to a bigger club

Robinson’s departure was confirmed yesterday in a statement.

Despite winning the League Cup with St Mirren a year ago and guiding the Buddies to three straight top-six finishes, he never enjoyed a consistent run of results at Pittodrie.

‘Following a board meeting, Aberdeen Football Club has today taken the decision to part company with head coach Stephen Robinson,’ the statement read.

‘Stephen leaves the club alongside assistant head coach Brian Kerr and first team coach Stephen Craigan. We thank them for their efforts during their time at Pittodrie and wish them every success for the future.

‘Preparatory work to support the appointment of a new manager has been undertaken, and the recruitment process is ongoing. The focus will be on identifying the right candidate to lead the club forward and deliver the progress expected across all areas of the football operation.

‘Stuart Duff will assume interim charge of first team training while the club continues its search for a new head coach.’

#2101. Come sta andando l'esperienza di Vincenzo Italiano da allenatore del Besiktas

L'ultima immagine di Vincenzo Italiano in Serie A è quella di un allenatore ormai conscio che il suo percorso al Bologna era finito. Lo avevano capito tutti, lui per primo, ma nessuno era abituato a vederlo senza la sua proverbiale grinta, quella fame con cui ha sempre guidato le sue squadre dalla panchina. In estate ha sfiorato il Napoli di Aurelio De Laurentiis, che ha però virato su Allegri e così lui ha scelto il Besiktas, accettando un contratto da 6 milioni di euro più bonus a stagione fino al 2028.

In Turchia il suo avvio è tutto sommato positivo, visto che è attualmente quarto in classifica. I punti di distacco dalla vetta occupata dal Galatasaray sono 6, con il Trabzonspor secondo a 3 lunghezze e l'Amed SK a +1. I presupposti sono ottimi.

Nella sua carriera ha ottenuto i risultati migliori nei tornei a eliminazione diretta, chiedere a Fiorentina e soprattutto Bologna in caso di dubbi. Nella seconda competizione più importante per club del nostro continente Italiano ha privilegiato il turnover, ma questo non gli ha impedito di essere momentaneamente all'8° posto, che significherebbe ottavi di finale senza passare dai playoff, con 7 punti in 4 partite. Il pubblico di Istanbul lo ha già adottato e il coro a lui dedicato ne è la testimonianza.

#2100. Stephen Robinson was just the latest Aberdeen manager to crash and burn... it's time to look at the club and its structure

By JOHN MCGARRY, SCOTTISH CHIEF SPORTS FEATURE WRITER

Published: 17:30 EST, 9 November 2026 | Updated: 17:30 EST, 9 November 2026

Across the space of two days last week, Aberdeen fans were dragged down memory lane only to emerge bruised and battered for their troubles.

Friday marked the 40th anniversary of plain old Alex Ferguson’s departure to Manchester United. It was an occasion for newspapers and TV bulletins to again recall the mind-blowing feats which led to the biggest club in Britain beating a path to Pittodrie.

Three Scottish championships, four Scottish Cups, a League Cup, the Cup Winners’ Cup and the Super Cup. The redrawing of the landscape of Scottish football.

There was something especially brutal about how those glories were juxtaposed beside the struggles of the soon-to-be former Dons boss, Stephen Robinson, 24 hours later.

After eight months of grim toil in the Granite City, the four-goal home thrashing the Northern Irishman suffered at the hands of Hibernian on Saturday blew away any lingering hope he retained of turning an increasingly desperate situation around.

A proven manager who was always simply the wrong fit for Aberdeen, Robinson will lick his wounds and come again.

Stephen Robinson is a proven manager who was always the wrong fit  for Aberdeen

But while a failure to live up to Fergie’s impossibly high standards might have done for those in Robinson’s shoes in the years which immediately followed the great man’s move to Old Trafford, no Aberdeen supporter in their right mind views things through that prism these days.

The majority of those who watched Saturday’s capitulation weren’t even born when Fergie’s Reds were running rampage throughout Europe.

They appreciate that the slayings of Real Madrid and Bayern Munich which they watched on YouTube were the highlights of a golden era for the club. One they know is not coming back.

In 2026, the expectation is more modest and hardly unreasonable. That with the fifth-highest average crowd in the country, Aberdeen should be in the top six almost every year, frequently playing in Europe most seasons and winning the odd cup. Yet, they’re miles off that right now.

While Jimmy Thelin did deliver a day in the sun by beating Celtic in the Scottish Cup final last year, that success was an anomaly. There have been just two top-six finishes in the past five years, with two ninth-placed finishes and one in tenth spot.

Given all that, it’s difficult not to recall the old wisdom about being careful what you wished for.

In Derek McInnes’ eight seasons at the club between 2013 and 2021, the team never finished below fourth and were runners-up four years on the spin. The League Cup was also secured in 2014.

A run of consistency and success which supporters had long craved was soon forgotten. McInnes’ familiarity and loyalty were eventually held against him.

Five managers later, Aberdeen appear to be as far away from reclaiming their status as one of the Scottish game’s powerhouses as they’ve ever been.

‘I think we’ve been waiting on it for four or five years,’ said Gothenburg legend Alex McLeish.

‘There’s been some signs, obviously winning the cup that year. You think that’s a wee springboard for the coming season and then it’s all kind of middle of the league, lower end of the league.’

As Robinson cleared his desk last night, even those fans who didn’t want him at the club in the first place would have been reluctant to hold him entirely responsible for the current ills.

The Northern Irishman is demonstrably a good manager. He guided St Mirren to three top-six finishes and the League Cup.

Thelin performed wonders across a long period at Elfsborg. Two years ago, he had Aberdeen sitting on top of the table.

Jim Goodwin was humiliated at Darvel, but now has buoyant Dundee United believing again.

Even Stephen Glass, a left-field choice to succeed McInnes, went on to enjoy success with Memphis 901 and as assistant to Robbie Keane at Ferencvaros.

When a club appoints one manager who doesn’t work out, it’s logical to question the individual. If five managers crash and burn, it says more about the environment and the structure they work in.

‘What is the solution?’ asked McLeish. ‘It’s definitely always down to what kind of players you’ve got.

Jimmy Thelin lifted the Scottish Cup before his Aberdeen career ended in disappointment

‘It’s trying to get a team to function together. To have forwards that you know can score, to have midfielders that can show a bit of quality, beat a man, playing one-twos, being accurate with your passing. Every bit of quality comes into it.

‘The manager will always get the worse side of it, you know, because they can’t sack 20 players.’

While it’s too late to save Robinson, the club do appear to have moved to address this particular shortcoming.

Back in March, Darren Mowbray returned to Pittodrie as head of recruitment three years on from his first stint which saw the captures of Bojan Miovski and Ylber Ramadani.

The trouble is that the mish-mash of players who were recruited by Jordan Miles, Chris Badlan and Nuno de Almeida are plainly not good enough.

Mowbray, it must be said, didn’t get too much right in the summer, either.

It’s not just the failure to sign players with the requisite pedigree and character which dismays the Aberdeen supporters.

When it was clear that Thelin’s time was up in January, the Swede was shown the door without there being a replacement waiting in the wings.

While there were legitimate reasons why Eirik Horneland couldn’t commit at that moment, the Dons’ hierarchy needed a Plan B.

Sandro Schwarz came to the Granite City for talks but nothing materialised, with Peter Leven remaining in interim charge.

With the pressure growing, Robinson – a wholly unpopular choice – was unveiled nine weeks and four days on. None of this reflected well on anyone involved.

Lutz Pfannenstiel, the sporting director, has written a book on his adventures in the game which included his recollections of the time he kept a penguin in his bath.

As Aberdeen’s hopes for the season have gone down the plughole, he’s not uttered a syllable in public.

Sporting director Lutz Pfannenstiel has had nothing to say during Aberdeen's recent troubles

With former FA bigwig Adrian Bevington now in the chief executive’s role, Pfannenstiel’s input into the recruitment of the new manager will be interesting.

And what role does Dave Cormack have to play? No one doubts the chairman’s passion for the club or his financial commitment to it.

But after seven years in the post, Pittodrie, on his watch, has acquired the unfortunate reputation of being a place where managers crash and burn. And you begin to suspect they might not be the sum total of the issues.

‘Dave Cormack is a close friend and I know he’s done everything in his power to try and get the Dons to a level,’ said McLeish. ‘We saw that Scottish Cup final victory a couple of seasons ago, but the league positions are not what Aberdeen fans expect.

‘Dave will be agonising. You know what it’s like if you get something wrong. You can’t sleep at night.

‘Dave will have been through all that kind of rigmarole as well. There’ll be a big burden on him right now.

‘He’s been stoic and he’s stood by the club. He has to have massive credit for that. It’s just about getting Aberdeen to cement their name back at the higher echelons of Scottish football.’

Alex McLeish was speaking at the announcement of William Hill as official sponsor of Walking Football Scotland. To find your nearest game visit walkingfootballscotland.org

#2098. Southampton will regret missing out on transfer deal for 25-year-old to Fulham: Opinion

Southampton were reportedly keen on luring the defender to St Mary’s in the last transfer window. However, they were beaten to the punch in the end by Premier League side Fulham.

Affengruber, 25, was snapped up by the London outfit on a four-year contract running until June 2028. He has since made seven league appearances for the Whites and has become a regular in their starting XI over recent weeks under the guidance of their head coach Alvaro Arbeloa.

The Austria international, who has three caps to his name so far in his career, was identified by the Saints as a potential replacement for Taylor Harwood-Bellis, who was sold to Aston Villa. However, a move to Hampshire wasn’t to be and Tonda Eckert’s side weren’t able to get a deal over the line in the end.

Southampton will rue not being able to land Affengruber following Keven Schlotterbeck’s recent injury that will see him ruled out for four to six weeks. They are now short of options at the back as they look to close the gap between them in the play-offs and the top two.

Southampton will have seen Affengruber as someone to bolster their defensive department. If he had joined them, he would have given them more competition and depth in his position.

The Saints will have to make do without Schlotterbeck over the coming weeks which isn’t ideal. Either Nottingham Forest loan man Zach Abbott or Nathan Wood will need to partner Jack Stephans in the heart of their backline.

Affengruber would have got plenty of game time under Eckert if he had linked up with the Championship side. He initially had to be patient at Fulham for his opportunity but he has now cemented his place in their team.

The Red Bull Salzburg academy graduate made his name at Sturm Graz. He then spent two years in Spain at Elche and played 84 games altogether, chipping in with two goals.

Southampton should sign a new defender in January. Affengruber is off the cards though after his recent emergence into the Fulham team.

Stephans isn’t getting any younger, Abbott could be recalled by Nottingham Forest and Wood has seen his minutes dry up. Therefore, they need someone to come in and perform next to Schlotterbeck when he returns.

The Saints lost 1-0 away at Preston North End last time out. They face Derby County at home after the international break.

#2096. ​Arteta’s ‘dynasty’ dream collides with Arsenal’s harsh reality

Mikel Arteta says he wants to create a “dynasty” at Arsenal as he threw down the gauntlet to his players to retain their Premier League title and reach a “different level”.

Arsenal are on the verge of completing a £75m deal for Brazil midfielder Bruno Guimãraes from Newcastle and have not given up hope of landing Real Madrid winger Vinícius Júnior, who has been offered an improved contract by the Spanish giants.

The Premier League champions slumped to a 3-1 defeat to Real Betis in Dublin on Wednesday night, when they paid the price for individual errors and a lethargic, sloppy defensive display.

But that did little to quell Arteta’s ambitions for the new season. “I’m even more excited [about this season], and we’re going to go and attack every possible title because that’s the ambition that we have,” he said.

“We know that we have the ability. We will go from a strength to a strength as well, whether it’s with the players that we have to take them to a different level, and with the market that is still open to recruit as well, so we are very optimistic about it.

“All the work that we’ve done – that I’ve done through the summer – is to understand what we need to do to win again and to create a dynasty in this football club. That’s the aim.”

Despite Arteta’s bullishness, he conceded Arsenal would have to be a lot better than they were against Betis. When asked about the mood in the dressing room, he said: “Very upset, given a hard time. They are fuming, but this is good. That’s what we need.”

Telegraph Sport analyses some of the talking points from a disappointing evening at the Aviva Stadium.

The importance of goalkeeper David Raya and centre-backs William Saliba and Gabriel Magalhães to Arsenal hardly needs overstating. Their title success was built on the foundations provided by that triumvirate at the back.

Raya has yet to report back from holiday after Spain’s World Cup triumph and Saliba is facing an extended spell on the sidelines with a back injury. Gabriel did appear for the second half but, watching this, Arteta is unlikely to have been dissuaded from the belief he needs another top defender to compensate for the loss of Saliba.

It may have been only Arsenal’s second game of pre-season, so a degree of legginess was understandable.

But the ease with which they were cut open and overloaded by the Spanish side, and the volume of individual errors, did not sit comfortably with Arteta with just over a fortnight until they kick off their title defence at home to newly promoted Coventry City.

Only once in 63 matches last season did Arsenal concede three goals in a single game but they were fortunate Betis did not score more. “In the first half there are two different things: to play well and to compete,” said Arteta, who saw his side trail 3-1 at the interval.

“We did the first one quite well in a lot of moments but the compete part – especially in and around our box – was not to the standards to win a football match against a good side. It’s better to give them [the goals away] in a friendly match than an official match. It’s always good when you lose a match in pre-season because it creates a bit more fire in the belly.”

Kepa Arrizabalaga will not want to watch Betis’s first goal back in a hurry. The Arsenal goalkeeper may have got a slight nudge as he came for the ball at a corner but he was all over the place and never looked even in the vaguest control of the situation. Rodrigo Riquelme scored at the far post after the ball sailed over the keeper. Arteta called it a “really sloppy goal”. With former Leeds goalkeeper Illan Meslier having arrived on a free transfer as back-up to Raya, Kepa faces an uncertain future at Arsenal. Moments like this will not help his cause.

He did not feature much last season but is probably best remembered for his torrid showing in the Carabao Cup final defeat by Manchester City in March. He looked as unconvincing here as he did then, as the former Chelsea goalkeeper also sliced a clearance in the build up to Betis’s third goal. Before then, Kai Havertz had turned over possession cheaply from which Nelson Deossa set up former West Ham midfielder Pablo Fornals to score.

Arsenal need more creativity and firepower in attack, hence the interest in Vinícius and Atlético Madrid’s Julián Alvarez. Viktor Gyokeres again looked rather limited here and the likes of Bukayo Saka and Eberechi Eze are also still to report back. Yet one of the few, brighter aspects of Arsenal’s performances was the sight of the regal looking Riccardo Calafiori marauding forward from a very nominal left-back role.

If Arsenal are going to play more adventurously this season and try to ratchet up things in the entertainment stakes then Calafiori could have a big role to play, as long as Arteta can keep him fit. He can be a potent weapon galloping forward and there were clear glimpses of that here. Harnessing that talent while not compromising too much defensive security may be one of the manager’s challenges.

Louie Copley had only been on the pitch for nine minutes – a half-time substitute for Ben White – when the 19-year-old midfielder went down largely unchallenged with what looked like a troubling knee injury. Arteta admitted afterwards that the way the teenager fell to the ground was a concern and he looked in visible pain and distress as he was treated by the medical staff before being helped off the pitch.

The fear will be of some kind of ligament damage and Arteta confirmed Copley would be scanned on Thursday. “Quite worried to be fair for the kid because the mechanism doesn’t look very positive and the symptoms that he already has,” the manager said. “So we are going to have to wait and see for tomorrow’s scan. Touch wood that is not a bad one.”

“It is another opportunity for fitness. Coming into games against teams of good quality, it is important we get good fitness. It is a chance to build momentum for the season ahead. We are taking steps in the right direction. Of course, we want more from the performance and the results, but we take learnings and move on.

“All of us, together, we share a common goal. We have confidence to build on. We will take every game as it comes. The atmosphere was amazing. I did not realise Ireland has so many Arsenal fans. We are so grateful for everyone who came to support us today. We wanted to give them a good result and performance. Hopefully next time we can.”

“We always want to win and in the end we could not today. In some ways they deserved it and we could have done better. We did have some good moments though. We had an amazing season last season. I believe in this team and there are still players to come back from the World Cup. I am confident but we can always do better.”

There is the final whistle in Dublin and Betis secure a 3-1 win over Arsenal at Aviva Stadium. Goals from Riquelme, Deossa and Fornals has led Manuel Pellegrini’s side to the victory. All four goals in the game came before the break.

And that's full time in Dublin 👏Real Betis claim a 3-1 win against Premier League champions Arsenal 🌴 pic.twitter.com/0XWByNdtgh

Jesus gets onto the end of a ball in behind, getting to it just before the byline, and delivers a dangerous cross but Natan does well on the stretch to clear.

Antony was booed when he came on and is also being booed whenever he touches the ball.

Arsenal have registered just two shots on target in this game and both of those came in the first half. They have not really looked like scoring since the break, with probably Jesus coming closest.

Double change for Betis as Morante and Gomez replace Riquelme and Bernal.

Bellerin squares the ball from the byline and picks out Riquelme near the penalty spot but the latter is unable to take advantage.

The changes keep coming for Arsenal as Gabriel and Martinelli, who have only recently returned from their breaks after the World Cup, go off after half an hour on the pitch. Nwaneri was the final member of the Arsenal starting XI to still be on but his night is over.

Up until this point, Manuel Pellegrini had made just one change but he now makes a triple substitution, which sees three former Premier League players come on. Former Leeds duo Firpo and Llorente come on as does ex-Manchester United forward Antony. There are a few boos from the Arsenal fans as Antony comes on.

Martinelli is played in down the left channel and gets onto the end of the ball inside the Betis box. He checks back and finds his compatriot Jesus on the edge of the box. Jesus tries to pick out the bottom corner but his shot goes just wide.

Isco should put Betis 4-1 up but tries to be too cute and clever. Former Arsenal man Bellerin delivers a cross from the right side of the box and picks out a free Isco in the six-yard box. Isco attempts an audacious flick rather than putting his foot through it and his effort is saved by Meslier. That should have been a goal.

Arsenal have changed nearly their entire starting XI whereas Betis have made just the one substitution thus far. Betis have played more games so far in pre-season, which probably explains the difference in the number of changes.

Salmon is deceived by a dummy by Riquelme and then brings the Betis man down, earning him a yellow card.

On the hour mark, Mikel Arteta is going to make a triple change, which feels like it was a plan going into the game. That includes Meslier coming on in goal in place of Kepa.

Copley is helped off by the two Arsenal medics, shaking his head and not putting any weight on his left leg, and is replaced by Julienne.

You have to hope that the knee injury to Louie Copley is not serious but judging by the way he went down largely unchallenged, his reaction and the way he was being helped down the tunnel it didn’t look too good. A tough moment for the youngster who had only been on the pitch nine minutes.

Play is halted as Copley has been down for a little while clutching at his left knee. He got his studs stuck in the ground and went down in some pain. The medics come on to check that left knee. He only came on at half-time but his night is over not even 10 minutes into the second half.

Nwaneri catches Bernal and if this was a competitive fixture than I think Nwaneri would perhaps have been booked for that challenge.

Those raft of changes from Mikel Arteta at half-time have seen just four of their starting XI remain on the pitch; Kepa, Calafiori, Lewis-Skelly and Nwaneri.

Arsenal have a corner early on in this second half as Vieira’s shot from distance deflects behind. The delivery from the left has too much on it, going over the heads of all the Arsenal players in the box, and Betis are able to clear.

Mikel Arteta has made a whole host of changes, which includes the likes of Jesus, Martinelli, Gabriel and Vieira coming on. Betis have brought the former Real Madrid man Isco on at the break.

Possession: Arsenal 63%-37% Real BetisShots: 5-7Shots on target: 2-6Corners: 1-2

Arsenal’s title triumph was built on that stout defence and conceding so few but there have been so many self-inflicted wounds here. The first and third goals came from dismal individual errors from Kepa and Havertz and the second - while a wonder strike - came after Arsenal had failed to clear their lines properly.

The first half is brought to an end and Real Betis take a 3-1 lead into the break.

Manuel Pellegrini’s side have their third goal of the night and restore their two-goal advantage. Havertz gives the ball away poorly deep inside his own half and the ball is played into Fornals. The former West Ham man has it on the edge of the box and has P Garcia in space to his right but opts to shoot. It is a clinical finish from Fornals as he picks out the bottom left corner and celebrates by crossing his arms to form irons, paying tribute to his days at West Ham.

"It's so so easy!" 🎙️The former West Ham man Pablo Fornals puts Real Betis 3-1 up against Arsenal 🌴🔨 pic.twitter.com/iyBeyS1nHd

Betis come close to restoring their two-goal advantage. Calafiori gives the ball away deep inside his own half and the ball is quickly moved onto P Garcia on the right side of the box. He opens up his body and curls a shot first time towards the far corner, forcing Kepa into the save down to his right.

Riquelme shoots from just outside the box and forces Kepa into a save down to his right.

Betis have one goal from a corner and now Arsenal do. Betis keeper Valles complains that he is being fouled by Havertz after Tzolis swung in the corner from the left and the ball comes through to Hincapie, who has the simplest of headers from inside the six-yard box.

Normal service is resumed with a Gunners goal from the corner 👏Piero Hincapié gives the @Arsenal fans reason to celebrate! 🔴 pic.twitter.com/b7h979i0Gv

Important block from P Garcia. Dowman cuts in from the right across the face of the penalty area and finds Tzolis in space to his left. Tzolis takes one touch before taking on the shot from inside the Betis box but P Garcia slides in with a crucial block.

That is quite the finish! Deossa receives the ball in a central position around 25 yards out. He takes a couple of touches before lashing a shot with his left foot. His effort flies into the top corner and Betis have a 2-0 lead. Considering the quality of the finish, I am surprised that Deossa did not celebrate more bearing in mind you do not score screamers like that every day!

Nelson Deossa...what a hit! 😱😱Real Betis double their lead over Arsenal with a stunning strike and Mikel Arteta doesn't look happy 🌴 pic.twitter.com/4J5QnjOury

Since Gyokeres misses that very good chance just a minute into the game, Arsenal have not really carved out many genuine opportunities in front of goal, other than that Tzolis wasted chance. Every time they reach the final third, they are lacking that cutting edge.

Calafiori’s role is interesting. He’s very nominally a left back but is routinely stepping into midfield and raiding forward, often ending up in positions you’d associate with a No. 10. It’s the kind of role Nico O’Reilly played under Pep Guardiola at Manchester City.

Valles is so, so lucky. The Betis keeper inexplicably passes the ball straight at Tzolis inside the Betis penalty area but Tzolis is unable to capitalise. That is a huge slice of luck for Valles as that was a ridiculous moment.

Betis have already scored one goal from a corner and they now have another corner after Nwaneri’s clearance behind. The inswinger is sent into a good area but this time Arsenal deal with the danger.

Arsenal were the better side in the early stages but Betis have grown into this and have taken confidence from that goal.

The first goal of the night goes the way of the Spaniards. The corner from the left should be dealt with by Kepa but the Arsenal keeper makes an absolute mess of it. The ball goes straight through him and comes to Riquelme at the far post. He takes one touch before firing into the net. Kepa is appealing for a free-kick but there is never a foul in there.

Real Betis strike first at the Aviva! 🌴Riquelme takes advantage of poor Arsenal defending at the corner to open the scoring 👀 pic.twitter.com/1ubVDayElP

That is dire goalkeeping from Kepa. He’s desperately tried to claim he was fouled but he just made a complete hash of it.

Betis have their first shot of the night. P Garcia receives it out on the right and cuts inside onto his left. The space opens up for him to shoot and he obliges, forcing Kepa into a save before the ball is smashed behind for a corner...

Calafiori may be on the teamsheet tonight for Arsenal at left-back but when they are in possession the Italian is moving into midfield. Lewis-Skelly, who is playing in the number six role, is dropping in between the two centre-backs when Arsenal have the ball.

Betis win a free-kick out on the left, with the chance to send it into the Arsenal penalty area for the first time tonight. It is clipped into a good area but Kepa is out of his goal to catch it.

Arsenal should be ahead one minute into the game with their first attack of the game. Havertz picks up the ball on the corner of the box out on the left and passes it inside to Gyokeres. The Swedish striker takes one touch before shooting from the edge of the six-yard box but his shot is saved. In reality, Gyokeres should not have given Valles in the Betis goal any chance of saving that one.

Both sides emerge from the tunnel, Arsenal led out by Ben White and Betis by former Arsenal man Hector Bellerin, and make their way past the Premier League trophy. We are moments away from kick-off in Dublin.

“When I see the training and how hungry they are for every training drill, that means everybody is ready to compete at the highest level. When you are a player of Arsenal, you compete for every title that is possible. It is not only about the Premier League, it is about all the competitions we are playing for. I see a clear mindset and everything, that everybody is on the same page to win titles here. I saw it from the first training and I think when you train like that in the game, it makes it even better. I came here ready, with my fitness level and I was happy that I could show it here as well. I want to be myself and to show on the pitch all the passion and desire to win because I really like to win games and to give everything.

“If I give 100% for every game and every duel, I think who does not like this? I think that is the best thing for a football player, and then you will always have the support from the supporters. I am thinking about how to improve and settle as fast as possible in the club, because I have the opportunity to train directly with the team from the beginning of the season, to play pre-season games and then to go to official games. This is a chance to play directly from the beginning and then it is up to me to show the qualities and what I can bring to the team. I am pretty confident I will show it in the next games.”

Arsenal are closing in on the signing of Newcastle and Brazil midfielder Bruno Guimaraes in a deal worth £75m. Our starting XI for #ArsenalRealBetis! 💚🤍 pic.twitter.com/8rRvy9IZCm

🟡 𝗧𝗘𝗔𝗠𝙉𝙀𝙒𝙎 🔵 Unchanged from last time out.Let's keep our good pre-season form going ✊🤝 Presented by @deel pic.twitter.com/S5TKqR9Lv3

Premier League champions Arsenal continue their preparations for the new season as they take on Spanish side Real Betis at the Aviva Stadium in Dublin, with this being the first-ever meeting between the two sides. This is the first time Arsenal have played a pre-season game in Dublin since August 2018, when they drew 1-1 with London rivals Chelsea, with Arsenal winning on penalties.

Arsenal beat Girona 4-1 in their opening game of pre-season, with new signing Christos Tzolis on the scoresheet in his first appearance for the club. After today, Arsenal have three more games, including the Community Shield, before they kick off the defence of their Premier League crown at home against newly-promoted Coventry on opening night of the new season. They are closing in on a deal for Newcastle captain Bruno Guimaraes, who is set to become one of Arsenal’s most expensive signings. Mikel Arteta’s side have been dealt a blow ahead of the season with the news that France centre-back William Saliba will miss an extended period due to a back injury. The likes of Declan Rice, Bukayo Saka and Noni Madueke are still yet to return after their World Cup exploits.

Real Betis, under the management of former Manchester City boss Manuel Pellegrini, finished fifth in La Liga last season and have therefore qualified for this coming season’s Champions League after a 20-year absence from the competition. Pellegrini has led Betis into European competitions for five consecutive seasons, which includes losing the 2025 Europa Conference League final to Chelsea. They have been busy in pre-season action already having played five games up to this point. They have won four of the five, including a 4-0 victory over Lyon, with just one defeat coming against Granada.

They have a number of former Premier League players in their ranks, including Antony, Giovani Lo Celso, Pablo Fornals and former Arsenal man Hector Bellerin. Real Betis have already played a number of friendlies as they build up to the start of their season, which begins on August 21 when they take on Real Sociedad.

#2095. La leggenda Scifo prima di Belgio-Italia: "Lukaku in forma è unico". Ed esalta Pio Esposito

"Sarà una partita dif­fi­cile per il Bel­gio, per­ché l'ita­lia sta cre­scendo bene e, vedendo le par­tite che ha gio­cato dopo l’esor­dio in Nations con­tro di noi, è miglio­rata tanto". A dirlo a La Gazzetta dello Sport è una leggenda del calcio belga come Vin­cenzo Scifo, ex di Inter e Torino e appunto simbolo dei Diavoli Rossi: "Man­cini sta facendo un lavoro molto inte­res­sante e gesti­sce bene il gruppo".

Parlando dei singoli azzurri, Scifo dedica parole di elogio all'uomo del momento in casa Italia, Pio Esposito: "Mi piace per­ché è molto intel­li­gente, sa quello che deve fare con e senza il pal­lone, aiuta la squa­dra a salire e in area c’è sem­pre. Alla squa­dra manca tanto un punto di rife­ri­mento come lui: se Romelu è in forma, la squa­dra gioca meglio", conclude Scifo.

#2094. Stoke City won’t regret selling 27-year-old in the summer transfer window: Opinion

Stoke City cashed in on the attacker on 1st September and he penned a four-year deal at the MKM Stadium running until June 2030. Since leaving SCFC, he has made six appearances for the Tigers in all competitions and has chipped in with one assist.

Thomas, 27, was a key player for the Potters during his time at the Bet365 Stadium and was one of their key players under Mark Robins. He made 52 outings for the Staffordshire outfit in all competitions, scoring 10 goals laying on 15 assists for his ex-teammates.

Robins’ side haven’t missed him at all though and they are sat joint-top of the Championship table along with Birmingham City and Middlesbrough. They are positioned in 3rd on goal difference behind Boro and they are in the play-offs along with Wolves, West Brom, Swansea City, Southampton and QPR after 15 fixtures.

Stoke City won 2-1 away at Portsmouth last time out before the international break after goals by Jack McGlynn and Ben Wilmot. They are back in action next weekend at home to Birmingham City.

Stoke City have flourished since Thomas’ departure. SCFC are less reliant on one player and it has become more of a team effort.

The Wales international, who has 31 caps to his name, hasn’t been able to make the impact he would have wanted to at Hull City. He missed out on their recent squads against Brentford and Ipswich Town and was left on the bench last Saturday against Arsenal.

Thomas faces a tough battle to get into the Tigers’ starting XI in the top flight. They are positioned in 13th in the league and have adapted pretty well to the step up a division against the odds.

Stoke City signed the London-born man back in 2025. Prior to his switch, he had permanent spells at Boreham Wood and Huddersfield Town, as well as loans at Blackburn Rovers and FC Nantes.

Thomas was a great player for Stoke City and his numbers were very impressive. He deserved a big move to Hull City off the back of his form at SCFC.

However, the Potters are better off without him now and have others players stepping up. Robins’ men will be full of confidence at the moment and will be aiming to keep their momentum going against Birmingham City next Saturday.

#2093. Journey to Wembley: Halifax set sights on winning more fans and climbing mountain to the Football League as their FA Cup adventure is ended by route-one Cheltenham

By IAN HERBERT AT EV CHARGER POINTS STADIUM

Published: 21:00 EST, 8 November 2026 | Updated: 18:05 EST, 9 November 2026

The FC Halifax manager John McGrath’s father-in-law is the top British mountaineer Alan Hinkes OBE and he knows that he has something of a summit of his own to scale, as he walks straight from the team bus to the pitch, set in the lee of Cheltenham’s mist-shrouded Cleeve Hill.

McGrath is all-too familiar with this setting. He brought his Buxton team here in the FA Cup's second round last season and was on the fringes of a major upset – 2-1 up at half-time – before watching the game fall away.

Cheltenham are a much stronger club, 12 months on, but our few weeks with McGrath, an Irishman who 25 years ago was playing for Aston Villa against Liverpool in the Premier League, reveal his ambition for the National League side he joined this summer.

We’re alongside him and FC Halifax on our journey to Wembley, told through all 13 rounds, following a club as far as they go and then picking up with the team who knock them out, all the way to the final.

A mist-veiled evening at Halifax’s Shay Stadium, a few weeks back, revealed much about 46-year-old McGrath. He arrives with his two-man coaching team for a Q&A with fans who, when the evening’s proceedings are finished, form a long and orderly queue to talk to him, one-on-one. The team’s four-hour drive to Southend awaits the following morning. But McGrath has no eye for the clock.

This managerial journey is one he hadn’t anticipated when, aged 32, a serious foot injury ended his long career with Burton Albion. He secured a master’s degree in broadcasting and media at Staffordshire University and had begun spots for Sky Sports and BBC local radio, yet here he is on ‘the grass’ he'd never wanted to leave.

His wife Fiona is Director of Public Realm at Islington Council and their time together is some weeks limited to a coffee at Doncaster station, near their home, when he drops her before daybreak. ‘It’s our little half an hour,’ he relates. ‘We’re ships that pass in the night.'

Cheltenham edged out FC Halifax 2-0 in front of 2,961 fans to reach round two

Cheltenham boss Steve Cotterill shakes hands with Halifax manager John McGrath

Ground staff put up the goalposts at EV Charger Points Stadium, also known as Whaddon Road

His club’s fans are partisan – 350 are here – and yearn for League Two, though there’s not a fortune at McGrath’s disposal. The club’s owner David Bosomworth was at the club when it went bust in 2008 and they say that experience left him painfully aware of the need for sustainability. Fair enough. Bosomworth has declined to speak to us. He is not at Cheltenham.

An hour out from kick-off, McGrath is handing out training vests for the warm-up as well as observing things – it’s a modest backroom team – and that defeat last season perhaps plays on his mind. ‘Cheltenham tweaked it that day and personally, I think I should have done the same,’ he says. ‘I’m a better manager, 12 months on.’

The club could badly use the £50,000 prize money that a win here would bring, not to mention the accompanying shot of exhilaration.

McGrath already belongs in the margins of the tournament’s history. He and his son, Jay, a Doncaster Rovers player, became the first father and son to play in the same team in an FA Cup game when McGrath, Mickleover Sports' player-manager for their preliminary-round win against Coventry Sphinx in 2019, fielded his 17-year-old boy.

Halifax seem up for this challenge. Their players go looking for their kit in their bus's luggage compartment before the driver has jumped out to produce the key. But Cheltenham have come a long way in the past year.

The former Burnley owner Mike Garlick bought the club 15 months ago and his investment strategy has echoes of the methods which made the Turf Moor club a Premier League side for seven out of eight years. Manager Steve Cotterill, quickly installed by Garlick, was briefly with him at Burnley.

Cheltenham fans arrive at the ground with their side riding high, fifth in League Two

Halifax fans cheer on their side but their Cup run is over - they travel to Kidderminster next Saturday in the National League

Mike Garlick bought Cheltenham last year and quickly installed Cotterill as manager

There’s an air of optimism around the place, as head groundsman Paul Gibson attends to some stray grass cuttings with a leaf blower and husband and wife team Gerry and Hannah Oldham sort the kit.

The ground staff, with their distinctive West Country burr, take enjoyment from several attempts at installing the goalposts before getting them upright. Mascot ‘Whaddney the Robin’ is limbering up for a leisurely lap of the stadium.

Garlick suggests we use Cotterill’s office as a quiet place to talk as the stadium begins to fill up. He tries calling the manager for his consent to do so but Cotterill’s phone rings out in the room.

It turns out Cotterill is receiving calls on his watch. This is a source of some humour as Cotterill eventually barrels into the office, exhibiting his usual energy. The two men clearly get on.

Extra preliminary round, August 8: Nelson 3-2 Stockport Georgians

Preliminary round, August 22: Witton Albion 1-0 Nelson

1st qualifying round, September 5: Avro 0-2 Witton Albion

2nd qualifying round, September 19: South Shields 7-0 Witton Albion

3rd qualifying round, October 3: Buxton 0-2 South Shields

4th qualifying round, October 17: Halifax 3-2 South Shields

1st round, November 7: Cheltenham Town 2-0 Halifax

Garlick looks more at home here than at Burnley. He also owns Spanish third-tier side Antequera CF and a club in Gibraltar but yearned for this kind of environment and Cheltenham, tumbling towards the EFL exit last year, badly needed help.

‘I missed English football,’ Garlick says. ‘I thought this was a club where I could make a difference and have some impact. When I came in, we had four points from nine games and were bottom of the league. It was hard work last season, but the fans seem happy for me to be here.

'Everyone who comes up to me says “thanks for helping the club,” which is nice. I never quite got that at Burnley to be honest. I’ve probably got more thanks here in 12 months than in 17 years on the board at Burnley. But that’s football isn’t it?!’

There’s been substantial investment in the training ground, a new stadium hospitality lounge, and Cotterill’s contacts have contributed to some eye-catching signings including veteran former Villa forward Andreas Weimann from Rapid Vienna.

Halifax start the game well, with wingers Ben Tollitt and Connor Douglas both flying and Conor Carty, the Irish forward who has been a revelation this season, ready to receive. Twice, they go close in the early stages. But Cotterill has a very uncomplicated route-one method in place and whip-fast striker Kirsten Otchere, on loan from Leicester City, looks lethal.

Will Flint’s string of fine interventions for Halifax is matched by the excellence of goalkeeper True Grant but Otchere crosses from the left for Jake Evans to score, left-footed.

This time McGrath does change things, adjusting the defensive line to quell Otchere’s threat after half time. Cotterill, whose colourful touchline antics include shoving his full-back out of the way when he blocks his line of view, grows concerned as Cheltenham’s attacking threat fades and Halifax dominate.

Carty nets from a Douglas’ crosses but is offside. Halifax lack the moment of class to convert superiority into goals. Substitute George Miller’s late header ended Halifax’s tournament for another year.

Halifax's Ben Tollitt battles for possession but his side cannot find a way through Cheltenham

McGrath and his Halifax players applaud the travelling supporters after their Cup exit

The Cheltenham fans flood out into suburban Whaddon Road fortified in their belief that something is building. ‘An FA Cup run is great for morale and also a massive contribution to the finances, because we’re hopefully going to strengthen again in January and the summer,’ says Garlick.

A foggy trip up the M6 awaits Halifax, though McGrath brings both a hinterland and perspective to the quest of start filling the famous old Shay Stadium once more and pushing closer to the fourth tier after 24 years away. His father-in-law was the first Briton to climb all the world's 8,000m mountains, in the Himalayan and Karakoram ranges of Nepal, Tibet and Pakistan.

‘He’s one of the best in the world,’ McGrath relates. ‘And however tough it is being a footballer or football manager, you try sitting on the side of Mount Everest for three months.

‘There’s a rumour building in our town that we play good football, that there’s goals to be seen at our stadium and a bunch of talented young men are representing the club in the right way.

'We want to get another 800 fans a week through the door and kick the club up the division and into the Football League. We think we can do that.’

READ MORE: Halifax reach first round in a beloved stadium they may soon lose

#2091. "Per 6 mesi non sarò calciatore": Kvaratskhelia diventa streamer, i momenti già virali

Durante il periodo di stop forzato a causa del grave infortunio, Khvicha Kvaratskhelia ha deciso di intraprendere una nuova attività sul web diventando uno streamer, su Twitch. Il georgiano ha avviato una serie di dirette per interagire con i propri sostenitori e magari ingannare il tempo in compagnia, durante il lungo percorso per tornare in campo.

La stella del Paris Saint-Germain ha condiviso con i propri followers dettagli riguardo ai tempi di recupero spiegando così la propria presenza sul web: "Per 6 mesi non sarò un calciatore", ha detto nel corso di una diretta. Nel corso delle sue trasmissioni in diretta non sono mancati momenti di condivisione e interazione diretta con i sostenitori, inclusi momenti di nostalgia come un abbraccio virtuale a un tifoso del Napoli che si è collegato.

Durante una delle sue dirette streaming ha ricevuto anche una telefonata inattesa da parte del compagno di squadra Achraf Hakimi. Kvara ha subito esaltato l'ex esterno dell'Inter davanti a tutti: "Questo è il miglior terzino sinistro della storia", ha detto del compagno. E ancora, una volta ha detto: "Il PSG è la squadra più forte di sempre, in campo e fuori".

#2090. 'I've never revealed the whole story': England fans wouldn't boo me if they knew the truth, says Ben White... as he defends World Cup walkout and rejecting call-ups

Published: 15:00 EST, 9 November 2026 | Updated: 18:20 EST, 9 November 2026

Ben White believes England fans would not boo him if they knew the truth behind his 2022 World Cup walkout.

The Arsenal defender left the camp in Qatar after the group stages citing 'personal reasons', although a fallout with Gareth Southgate's assistant, Steve Holland, was later reported. He then declined a return to the England set-up while Southgate and Holland were in charge but was included by Thomas Tuchel in March of this year.

However, White was booed by fans inside Wembley when he came on in the second-half of a friendly against Uruguay, only to score his first international goal and then concede a late penalty in a 1-1 draw. He was not selected for the World Cup in North America but is back with the squad ahead of this week's Nations League fixtures against Croatia and Spain.

And the 29-year-old, addressing those jeers for the first time, said: 'It was hard to hear, especially because they didn't know the situation, which is kind of what I put my mind to.

'They don't know my situation and it is not really about what I have done, because they don't know why, and if they did they would not be booing or saying these things. I just think about maybe (them) not knowing the full story, and if they did, maybe they wouldn't (boo).'

He added: 'Obviously, a load has been said. I've probably seen all of it. I just think people don't know the full story, because I've never said anything, and I won't put anyone under the bus or share any of that.

Ben White has insisted he would not be booed by England fans if they knew the truth behind his World Cup walkout

White fell out with former England assistant Steve Holland (left) before heading home during the 2022 World Cup

'There were loads of things that happened and communication wasn't great. I don't want to throw anyone under the bus. A lot of people try to do that to me so it's something I wouldn't want to do myself. I think it's probably the best way. It's a bit tricky and not straightforward, but it's amazing to be here now. It's a privilege to be called up.'

White, however, is adamant that he would make the same decision today and leave the World Cup, even if he will not divulge the reasons as to why.

'I don't think I would (do anything different),' he said. 'I think, as a person, I'm quite real, and I made the decision that I would probably continue to make. It's one that I'm not happy about. It wasn't ideal. But it happened, it's time to move on.

'I don't think I am looking to win anyone over or say anything that helps them get back on my side. It is what it is. There are reasons for it and I am just looking forward to playing for England and doing well and looking for success.'

White has spoken publicly about not watching football, and this, too, has been used against him by some supporters.

'It doesn't mean I don't like football, because I do it every day, so I must like it a little bit!' he said. 'It's because I don't watch it, which I think is quite normal for someone that does a job and then doesn't go home and then continue watching that job.

'I don't think anyone knows my personality. I keep that to my loved ones and family. It's all noise when people have an opinion on that stuff. It is not actually me. People don't know me and I like it that way. You give so much to the game, which is intense. I think it's important to keep some bits for yourself. I keep the energy for the people I love.'

England new boy Josh King believes his aptitude for academia gives him an advantage at the start of a burgeoning career.

The Fulham star – who has two Premier League goals this season – linked up with Thomas Tuchel’s squad on Monday morning as one of two fresh call ups alongside Jobe Bellingham.

King was a straight-A student at the renowned independent Hampton School in south London, with alumni including Queen guitarist Sir Brian May and golf pro Paul Casey.

The teenager’s mother, Michelle, is a university lecturer and finishing a PhD on sickle cell disease while his father Steve is a personal injury lawyer.

‘The most important thing about being good academically and education-wise is you’re able to take on information much more quickly,’ King said.

‘You can take on information which might take a bit longer for other people. I think that definitely helps.’

While the 19-year-old was invited to take part in a pre-World Cup training camp in Miami, he is hoping to make his international bow against Croatia on Thursday.

Although a central figure in Fulham’s midfield, a confident King could be utilised wider by Tuchel were he to be given an opportunity across the two-game Nations League camp.

‘There was a buzz around Fulham when I got called up [this week],’ he added. ‘I got off the phone with the boss, ran into the kitchen and started cheering with my dad. I called mum at work and she was screaming in the office. This felt slightly unachievable when I was younger.’

Jude Bellingham and Harry Kane were namechecked as those who helped him settle when introduced to the group in Florida, with King having also studied the likes of Jamal Musiala and Pedri to elevate his own game.

‘The main thing you take is the standards they set,’ he said when reflecting on joining the camp in America. ‘The way they conduct themselves, the way they perform every day, the way they train and the way they take everything seriously.

‘The little things, technical things, the way some people strike the ball, the way some people pass, just little things which you almost take without thinking about it - they also have an impact.

‘On a few occasions I asked “how do you finish this way” or “what do you do in this scenario” and it always helps to add to my game.’
